Future Thinking and Depression retrieval is more likely to yield specific memories than generative retrieval (e.g., Williams et al., 2006), we did not find main effects of retrieval process on specific and categoric future thoughts. Considering cue valence and depressive symptoms, retrieval process appears to be independent of future thinking specificity, at least in this study.

Study 2

Study 2 was pre-registered and introduced several modifications to the measures used in Study 1, with the aim of replicating and extending the previous findings. Specifically, using a relatively larger sample size, Study 2 was conducted to test the two hypotheses examined in Study 1-namely, that depressive symptoms were associated with a greater number of direct retrievals of categoric future events and a smaller number of direct retrievals of specific future events. In addition, we changed the method for assessing contrast avoidance. Given that Study 1 found no association between the CAQ-W and future specificity, we replaced it with the CAQ-GE as the trait measure. Whereas the CAQ-W focuses on worry-a characteristic more closely associated with anxiety-the CAQ-GE measures the maintenance of negative emotions in general as a form of contrast avoidance, which may be suitable for examining contrast avoidance related to depression (Llera & Newman, 2017). Additionally, we developed two trial-level measures of contrast avoidance: Making Positive Contrasts and Negative Contrast Avoidance. Second, we replaced the Rehearsal item in Study 1 with Future Similarity item in reference to recent studies (McClure et al., 2024). This change allowed us to test the memory of the future hypothesis more precisely as the new item related to how similar future thoughts were to previous future thoughts, rather than to past events.

Hypotheses 1 and 2 were identical to those in Study 1.

Method Participants and Sample Size Determination

Our target sample size was 117 participants. In Study 1 (N = 80), we obtained r = .321 between depressive symptom severity and direct retrieval of categoric future thinking for negative cues, and r = -.256 between depressive symptom severity and direct retrieval of specific future thinking for positive cues. We aim to detect the latter as a smaller effect size. Using G*Power, we determined that N = 117 would provide 80% power to detect r = .256 at the standard .05 alpha error probability. We recruited 150 participants via Crowdworks, assuming some dropouts. Of these, 37 participants were Future Thinking and Depression excluded: 6 for being aged 36 or older, 28 for making omission errors more than half of trials in future thinking task, 2 for failing attention checks, and 1 for a technical error during the experiment (requiring the experiment to be restarted). The final sample size was N = 113 (Mage = 30.35±4.37, 52 males, 60 females, 1 other), which achieved power = 0.78, slightly lower than anticipated. There was no overlap of participants between Study 1 and Study 2. The mean years of education for the participants was 14.92. Among the participants, 65 were full-time workers, 33 were part-time workers, and 15 were not employed.

Future Thinking Task

The task and cue words were identical to Study 1.

We used the same questions as Study 1 for Vividness/Detail, Likelihood, Importance, Emotional Valence, Emotional Intensity, and Past similarity. The measure of Future Similarity was included instead of Rehearsal in Study 1: Future Similarity: How exactly similar was the future event you imagined compared to other future thoughts you have had before? (1: not at all, 7: very). In addition, the measures of Making Positive Contrasts and Negative Contrast Avoidance were included as follows for each cue word: Making Positive Contrasts: Does imagining the possibility of this future event coming true help you feel more positive emotions when you experience good things in the future? (1: not at all, 7: very), Negative Contrast Avoidance: Does imagining the possibility of this future event coming true prevent negative emotions from suddenly arising when you experience bad things in the future?

(1: not at all, 7: very). The interrater agreement on the classification of future specificity was acceptable (Cohen's k = .57). Raters discussed and reached upon final coding.

Questionnaires

Depressive symptoms were again assessed with the DASS-D (doubled raw score), and contrast avoidance with the CAQ-GE. The CAQ-GE consisted of 25 items, which is rated on a 5-point scale (1 = not at all true,5 = absolutely true). The CAQ-GE encompassed two sub-factors: Factor 1 measures creating and sustaining negative emotion to avoid negative contrasts (e.g., I try to stay focused on the bad things that could happen, because it prevents me from feeling emotionally vulnerable) and the Factor 2 measures discomfort with emotional shifts (e.g., Strongly fluctuating emotions are particularly unpleasant for me). These scales demonstrated good internal consistency (DASS-D: α = .91; CAQ-GE F1: α = .94; CAQ-GE F2: α = .88).

Future Thinking and Depression

Results

Descriptive Statistics and Preliminary Analysis

Descriptive statistics are shown in Table 4. Specific future thinking was reported in 54.7% of all trials, and 59.4% when omissions were excluded. Omissions were observed in 7.9% of trials. Among trials excluding omissions, direct retrieval occurred in 75.6% (generative retrieval in 24.4%) of cases. Retrieval latency was significantly shorter for direct retrieval (M = 4.45 sec, SD = 4.11) than for generative retrieval (M = 7.26 sec, SD = 6.28), t = 9.52, p < .001, d = 0.59.

Correlations

Table 4 also shows correlations between depressive symptom severity, contrast avoidance, and future specificity variables. We confirmed that depressive symptom severity was positively associated with directly retrieved categoric futures for negative cues (r = .39, p < .001) and negatively associated with directly retrieved specific futures for positive cues (r = -.29, p = .002). Depressive symptom severity also negatively correlated with directly retrieved specific futures for negative cues (r = -.43, p < .001) 4.

Other correlations with depressive symptom severity were not significant (ps > .16).

Table 4. Descriptive statistics and correlations in Study 2 (N = 113) 4 After the FDR correction across the full set of 27 correlations reported in Table 4, all three correlations reported above remained significant: directly retrieved categoric futures for negative cues, pFDR < .001; directly retrieved specific futures for positive cues, pFDR = .006; directly retrieved specific futures for negative cues, pFDR < .001.

Future Thinking and Depression

Generalized Mixed-Effects Model for Future Specificity

To further validate the correlational findings, we tested generalized mixed-effects models for specific and categoric future thinking, entering cue valence, retrieval process, depressive symptom severity, and their two-and three-way interactions as predictors. For categoric future thinking (vs. other types of responses), the model revealed a significant main effect of depressive symptom severity (OR = 1.05 [95%CI: 1.01-1.10], p = .015), indicating that greater depressive symptom severity was associated with a higher overall probability of categoric future thinking.

The model also revealed a two-way interaction between retrieval process and depressive symptoms (OR = 1.07, p = .01) and a three-way interaction between cue valence, retrieval process, and depressive symptoms (Table 5; OR = 0.91, p = .046). Supporting Hypothesis 1, post-hoc analysis revealed that depressive symptom severity was associated with a greater probability of directly retrieved categoric future thinking for negative cues (Z = 5.35, p < .001; Figure 2A). The other simple slopes of depression, including in generative retrieval for negative cues and in direct retrieval for positive cues, did not reach significance (all ps > .07). For specific future thinking (vs. other types of responses), the model similarly revealed a significant main effect of depressive symptom severity (OR = 0.94 [95%CI: 0.90-0.98], p = .003), indicating that greater depressive symptom severity was associated with a lower overall probability of specific future thinking. A three-way interaction between cue valence, retrieval process, and depressive symptoms

A) B) *** *** *

Linear Mixed-Effects Model for Phenomenological Characteristics

We fitted linear mixed-effects models to test whether cue valence, retrieval process, depressive symptom severity, and their interactions predict the characteristics of future thinking (Table 6). Here, 2,116 trials, excluding omissions, nested within 113 participants were subject to the analyses. Other analysis settings were the same as in Study 1.

Only significant results related to depression are summarized here for the sake of brevity. Full results are shown in the Supplementary file. Results showed a main effect of depression on emotional valence (b = -0.03, p = .001), suggesting that depressive symptom severity was associated with higher negatively-valenced (or lower positively-valence) future thinking. We also found significant interaction effects between cue valence and depression on vividness (b = -0.06, p < .001), temporal distance (b = 0.04, p = .006), likelihood (b = -0.07, p < .001), emotional valence (b = -0.04, p = .002), importance (b = -0.04, p = .002), future similarity (b = -0.05, p = .002), and past similarity (b = -0.04, p = .044). Post-hoc analyses revealed that depressive symptoms were associated with lower vividness (t = -2.46, p = .015), lower likelihood (t = -3.87, p < .001), lower positive valence (t = -5.60, p < .001), lower past similarity (t = -2.08, p = .039) in positive cues (Figure 3). In contrast, these relationships were not evident in negative cues (ps > .24). For temporal distance, importance, and future similarity, there were no significant simple slopes of depressive symptoms as a function of cue valence (ps > .08). Instead, cue positivity was related to temporally closer (t = -3.25, p = .001), important (t = 10.40, p < .001), and previously thought (i.e., high future similarity; t = 5.74, p < .001) future thinking in lower levels of depression, and more important future thinking in higher levels of depression (t = 4.38, p < .001). There were no effects of cue positivity on temporal distance (t = 0.48, p = .63) and future similarity (t = 0.86, p = .39) in higher levels of depression.

Contrast Avoidance

Regarding trait contrast avoidance, correlational analyses (Table 4) showed that CAQ-GE Factor 1 (i.e., Creating and Sustaining Negative Emotion to Avoid Negative Contrasts) was positively correlated with direct retrieval of categoric futures for negative cues (r = .34, p < .001) and negatively correlated with direct retrieval of specific futures for positive cues (r = -.36, p < .001). The CAQ-GE Factor 1 also positively correlated with directly retrieved categoric (r = .21, p = .025) and negatively correlated with directly retrieved specific futures for negative cues (r = -.33, p < .001).

The CAQ-GE Factor 2 negatively correlated with directly retrieved specific futures for positive cues (r = -.22, p = .02) and positively correlated with generatively retrieved categoric futures for negative cues (r = .19, p = .041).

Regarding contrast avoidance for specific future thinking, and following the pre-registered exploratory analysis, we adopted linear mixed-effects models that used *** * *** * Future Thinking and Depression trial-level contrast avoidance scores as the dependent variables and entered future specificity (specific or categoric), cue emotional valence (positive or negative), depressive symptoms, and these interactions. A model of positive contrast yielded significant main effects of cue valence (b = 2.36, p < .001) and depressive symptoms (b = -0.03, p = .049), suggesting that positive cues and lower levels of depression were related to greater making positive contrasts (Table S1). No other significant two-way and three-way interactions were found (ps > .06). Results of negative contrast avoidance showed a significant two-way interaction between cue valence and depressive symptoms (b = -0.03, p = .041). Post-hoc analysis demonstrated that lower depressive symptoms were associated with higher negative contrast avoidance in positive cues (t = -2.77, p = .006; Figure S14). No significant association between depressive symptoms and negative contrast avoidance was observed in negative cues (t = -0.60, p = .55).

Notably, multilevel correlational analyses (Kenny & La Voie, 1985) demonstrated that CAQ Factor 1 did not significantly correlate with trial-level of making positive contrasts (mr = -.17, p = .16) and negative contrast avoidance (mr = -.04, p = .69), while CAQ Factor 2 correlated with trial-level of making positive contrasts (mr = -.34, p = .007) and negative contrast avoidance (mr = -.29, p = .008).

Unexpectedly, these negative, significant correlations indicated that individuals with higher levels of contrast avoidance thought that future thinking did not help making positive contrasts and avoiding negative contrasts. This raises questions about the validity of contrast avoidance measures at the trial-level.

Exploratory Analysis

To examine whether depressed individuals report lower likelihood, emotional valence, and similarity to past events because they have difficulty generating positive specific future events, or whether such reductions in hedonic responses persist even when specific future events are successfully generated, we conducted additional analyses restricted to trials in which specific future events were produced. These analyses included 1,236 trials nested within 112 participants.

Among the variables for which an interaction between cue valence and depressive symptoms was observed in the original analyses, the interactions were no longer significant for emotional valence (b = -0.01, p = .50), future similarity (b = -0.02, p = .07), and past similarity (b = -0.02, p = .15). In contrast, the interaction remained significant for vividness (b = -0.02, p = .008), temporal distance (b = 0.02, p = .037), likelihood (b = -0.03, p = .001), and importance (b = -0.03, p = .001). Simple slopes analyses revealed a significant effect of depressive symptoms on likelihood in positive Future Thinking and Depression cues (t = -2.07, p = .04). However, no significant effects of depressive symptoms in positive cues were observed for vividness (t = -1.24, p = .21), temporal distance (t = 0.89, p = .37), or importance (t = -1.37, p = .17).

In summary, except for a modest effect on likelihood, the reduction in hedonic responses typically observed in depressed individuals disappeared when analyses were restricted to specific future events. These findings suggest that many of the subjective reports observed in depression may arise from difficulty in generating positive specific future events, rather than a generalized reduction in hedonic responses per se.

Brief Discussion

In support of Hypothesis 1, GLMM demonstrated that depressive symptom severity was associated with a greater number of directly retrieved categoric futures for negative cues. Contrary to Hypothesis 2, the association between depressive symptoms and directly retrieved specific futures for positive cues was not established in GLMM, possibly due to the strong association between depressive symptom severity and fewer directly retrieved specific futures for negative cues hindering a specific effect of positive cues in direct retrieval. Indeed, we found significant bivariate correlations between depressive symptom severity and reduced directly retrieved specific futures for positive cues, as well as for negative cues. Instead, the GLMM revealed a significant simple slope for predicting fewer specific futures in generative retrieval for positive cues in depressed individuals, although this association was not established in a bivariate correlation.

We also found that contrast avoidance traits were associated with more directly retrieved categoric futures for negative cues and fewer directly retrieved specific futures for positive cues. This suggests that depressed individuals may view negative categoric future thinking as useful for avoiding negative emotional contrasts. Conversely, they may perceive positive specific future thinking as offering less benefit in maximizing the positive impact of future events.

With regard to phenomenological characteristics, depression-related effects were most evident for positive cues. For positive cues, depressed individuals demonstrated lower positive emotional valence, likelihood, and past similarity, suggesting that they have difficulty in retrieving possible positive futures because they lack relevant referent memories from the past. Whereas positive futures among nondepressed individuals tend to be pre-made, the positive futures of depressed individuals may not be, which may manifest as reduced generatively retrieved specific futures for positive cues. Interestingly, the exploratory analysis suggested that these Future Thinking and Depression reductions in hedonic responses among depressed individuals may be attributable to difficulty in generating positive specific future events. Conversely, this finding provides support for interventions aimed at enhancing the specificity and detail of positive future thinking in depression (e.g., Future Event Specificity Training; Hallford et al., 2020 Hallford et al., , 2023)).

General Discussion

This study examined the depressive psychopathology severity in future thinking, focusing on direct and generative retrieval processes. We also investigated potential mechanisms underlying altered future thinking, namely the memory of the future and contrast avoidance. In Study 1, we conducted a preliminary experiment to estimate effect sizes and in Study 2 we pre-registered and replicated the key findings in Study 1.

Our main contributions to the literature are (a) providing evidence that depressed individuals tend to directly retrieve negative categoric future events, and (b) showing that contrast avoidance may be associated with the functional aspects of altered future thinking accessibility. Hypothesis 1 was clearly supported in Study 2: depressive symptom severity was consistently associated with more frequent direct retrieval of categoric futures in response to negative cues, with no such association for generative retrieval or positive cues. These results suggest that in individuals with elevated depressive symptoms direct accessibility to negative categoric futures is selectively heightened among potential retrieval competitors that may come to mind (Brewin, 2006). In Study 2, depressive symptoms were also linked to fewer specific future events directly retrieved for negative cues, indicating that the preferential retrieval of negative categoric futures may mask access to competing specific future thoughts, thereby reducing negative future specificity.

These findings extend previous work on autobiographical memory, which has shown increased direct retrieval of categoric memories for negative cues in depression (Matsumoto et al., 2020 , 2023 , Hallford & Matsumoto, 2022) to the domain of future thinking. Direct retrieval of categoric future events in response to negative cues may reflect the projection of accumulated negative categoric memory representations into the future through associative retrieval. For example, an individual who frequently activates the categoric memory I have always failed may readily imagine the categoric future I will fail again in the future. In depressed individuals, such autobiographical memories may contribute to pessimistic cognitions and predictions about the future, although this study did not directly assess links with autobiographical memories.

Future Thinking and Depression Hypothesis 2, depressive symptom severity was associated with lower frequency of specific future thinking directly retrieved for positive cues, was not well supported in GLMMs across both studies. Nonetheless, the GLMMs for specific future thinking revealed a significant main effect of depressive symptom severity in both Study 1 and Study 2, indicating that, irrespective of cue valence or retrieval process, greater depressive symptom severity was associated with a lower overall frequency of specific future thinking. Instead of the hypothesized pattern, the GLMM in Study 2 revealed that depressive symptom severity was associated with less frequent specific future thinking retrieved generatively for positive cues. In the correlational analyses, however, this association was not significant; instead, depressive symptom severity was associated with less frequent specific future thinking retrieved directly for positive cues, consistent with Hypothesis 2. This inconsistency suggests that caution is warranted in interpreting whether direct or generative retrieval is impaired when depressed individuals imagine specific future thoughts in response to positive cues. This may partly arise from low depressive symptoms in the current sample, as clinical depression is characterized by diminished and dampened positive emotions (Bylsma et al., 2008; Vanderlind et al., 2020), and therefore this association may be more pronounced in more severely depressed populations. Previous studies suggest that reduced future specificity in depression may be particularly evident for positive cues (Gamble et al., 2019; Hallford et al., 2020; MacLeod, 2025), but the association may be less pronounced in relatively mild depression, such as in this study.

Further insights can be drawn from parameters related to the characteristics of future thinking. In both Studies 1 and 2, depressed individuals had lower positive emotional valence of future thinking and tended to underestimate the likelihood of positive events. In Study 2, lower similarity between positive future thinking and past events was also linked to depression, suggesting a scarcity of positive autobiographical memories to draw upon when imagining the future. Study 2 also showed that healthy individuals think about positive future events in advance (i.e., high future similarity), whereas depressed individuals did not. These results may be understood in the context of findings that individual experiencing dysphoria report thinking more about the future in general, but this future thinking is perceived as more negative, trivial, unrealistic, and unimportant (Hallford, 2019). Further, the reported function of future thinking in dysphoria is more likely to be about boredom reduction and preparing for negative events (Hallford & D'Argembeau, 2022). Therefore, while elevated depressive symptoms may be associated with more frequent future thinking, these future thoughts may not have the same utility of being remembered and retrieved for later use when Future Thinking and Depression simulating positive future events. In healthy individuals, memories of the positive future may have adaptive value, such as contributing to planning for the realization of those events (Schacter et al., 2017; Szpunar et al., 2013), whereas this function may be attenuated in depressed individuals. These deficits of pre-constructed positive futures may manifest as reduced retrieval of specific positive futures (Studies 1 and 2) and reduced vividness (Study 2). Consequently, lower positive emotional valence of positive futures was observed, and even if these futures can be generatively retrieved (Study 1), perceived likelihood may be underestimated (Studies 1 and 2).

In Study 1, depressive symptoms were associated with lower similarity between negative futures and past events, suggesting that depressed individuals generate many negative possibilities they have never experienced, or do not remember experiencing. However, in Study 2, similarity between negative future thinking and previously imagined futures (i.e., memory of the future) was unrelated to depressive symptoms, contradicting our initial prediction. Instead, it may be more accurate to interpret that depressed individuals use negative categoric futures as semantic scaffolds (Irish & Piguet, 2013), flexibly combining abstract knowledge to imagine diverse negative things.

Although Study 1 suggested that higher levels of depressive symptoms were associated with greater temporal distance, this association was not replicated in Study 2.

Instead, Study 2 showed that individuals with lower levels of depressive symptoms tended to imagine positive future events as temporally closer (and negative future events as more distant). Taken together, these findings suggest that healthy individuals are more likely to imagine positive events occurring in the near future, whereas this tendency is diminished in individuals with elevated depressive symptoms. A lack of near-future positive events may contribute to reduced behavioral activation and fewer opportunities for social reward, which have been proposed as key mechanisms in the maintenance of depression (Hallford, 2026; but see Hallford, 2025).

Taken together, these results suggest distinct mechanisms for reduced future specificity for positive versus negative cues in depressed individuals. Depression is characterized by increased accessibility to negative categoric future thinking, particularly via direct retrieval, and by reducing generative retrieval or broader reduction in accessibility to positive specific future thinking. Increased accessibility to negative categoric future events promotes generalized negative predictions, even for events never personally experienced, while reduced accessibility to positive specific futures limits the emotional benefits of anticipating positive events (Rizeq, 2024).

Study 2 further revealed that CAQ-GE scores, reflecting contrast avoidance via Future Thinking and Depression the maintenance of negative emotions, were associated with both greater direct retrieval of negative categoric futures and fewer direct retrievals of positive specific futures. This pattern supports the idea that contrast avoidance may sustain these retrieval biases.

Individuals with high contrast avoidance traits may tend to weaken emotional contrast arising from various types of negative future events (i.e., minimizing prediction error) by making general (categoric) rather than specific predictions about negative futures.

These notions are consistent with previous findings that depressed individuals tend to use future thinking to prepare for negative events and to regulate negative emotions (Hallford et al., 2022). In contrast, they may avoid imagining positive specific futures in advance to maximize positive contrast when good events occur. These findings suggest that contrast avoidance may serve as a maintaining or amplifying factor in depressionrelated changes in the accessibility of future thinking.

Notably, these associations were relatively stronger for CAQ-GE Factor 1 (Creating and Sustaining Negative Emotion to Avoid Negative Contrasts) and weaker or non-significant for Factor 2 (Discomfort with Emotional Shifts). Although the constructs underlying these two factors overlap, Factor 2 is thought to reflect sensitivity or vulnerability to emotional prediction error, whereas Factor 1 reflects a cognitiveaffective strategy aimed at minimizing such prediction error (i.e., maintaining a negative mood state; Llera & Newman, 2017; Llera et al., 2026). The results of Study 2 suggest that the coping strategy used to manage emotional vulnerability, rather than vulnerability to abrupt negative emotional shifts itself, is associated with reduced positive specific future thinking and increased negative categoric future thinking.

However, these findings regarding the CAQ-GE should be interpreted with caution, given that a related but distinct construct, namely CAQ-W (contrast avoidance with worry), was not significantly associated with future specificity in Study 1.

Nevertheless, the inconsistency may be explained by differences in the constructs measured by the CAQ-W and CAQ-GE. In Study 1, CAQ-W (contrast avoidance with worry) was not significantly associated with future specificity. While the CAQ-W measures characterize generalized anxiety disorder, CAQ-GE may be applicable to a wide range of mental disorders, including depression. Evidence does not support a clear relationship between anxiety and future specificity (Du et al., 2022), but there is robust evidence in the association between depression and reduced future specificity (Hallford et al., 2018; Gamble et al., 2019). While worry may involve general prospection of the future, intrusive images that arise during worry (Borkovec et al., 2004) may directly lead to specific negative futures, which could explain the lack of association between CAQ-W and negative future specificity. Moreover, as worry typically focuses on Future Thinking and Depression negative content (Borkovec et al., 2004), it may have little impact on positive future accessibility. Potentially, this association is more complex. Although anxiety is not clearly associated with specificity, it is related to more detailed future thinking in response to negatively-valenced cues, but lower detail for positively-valenced cues (Du et al., 2022). An alternative explanation for our main findings, independent of contrast avoidance, is mood-congruent processing (Faul & LaBar, 2023). As also observed in the present study, individuals with higher depressive symptoms show reduced positive specific future thoughts, which may reflect a diminished benefit from positive moodcongruent processing relative to nondepressed individuals. Similarly, the increased direct retrieval of negative episodic future events observed in the GLMM analyses of Study 2 may be interpreted in terms of enhanced negative mood-congruent processing in depression.

However, mood-congruent processing alone may not fully account for the increased direct retrieval of negative categoric future thinking. Specifically, it remains theoretically unclear how a negative mood state would preferentially facilitate not only specific future representations but also more generalized, categoric forms of future thinking. This mechanism therefore warrants further investigation in more controlled experimental settings.

The findings of this study are limited in several respects. First, as the sample consisted of Japanese crowd workers and depressive symptom scores were lower than those typically observed in clinically depressed populations, the generalizability of the findings to clinical populations, as well as to other populations and cultures, remains limited. Second, some differences emerged between Studies 1 and 2 in depressionrelated phenomenological characteristics, partially due to the exploratory nature and smaller sample size of Study 1. Therefore, future studies should replicate the findings of this study with larger, more diverse samples, including clinical depression. Third, we assessed direct vs. generative retrieval process via a single self-report question, following previous studies (e.g., Jeunehomme & D'Argembeau, 2016). However, several alternatives have been proposed to distinguish these two retrieval processes, including retrieval effort (Barzykowski et al., 2021), retrieval automaticity (Mace et al., 2021), and model-based approaches (Matsumoto et al., 2026a). Future studies should also consider these alternative approaches. Finally, as is the case in most future thinking studies using the Future Thinking Task, the potential dependency between the frequencies of specific and categoric (or general) future responses may pose a limitation in the present study. Model-based approaches may be particularly promising for Future Thinking and Depression disentangling the accessibility of specific and categoric future thinking (Matsumoto et al., 2026a).

Despite the above limitations, the findings of this study add a new perspective of emotional valence and retrieval process to the literature of future thinking in depression. While robust associations have been established between depression and future specificity (Hallford et al., 2018; Gamble et al., 2019), the differences in mechanisms between positive versus negative future thinking have been largely overlooked.

This study initially demonstrated that a finding from autobiographical memory specificity research on depression, increased direct retrieval of negative categoric memories (Matsumoto et al., 2026a), also apply to future thinking.

Furthermore, depressed individuals showed difficulty in the generative retrieval of positive specific future thinking, or in its accessibility more broadly, and were less likely to imagine such events as occurring in the near future, as likely to occur, or as highly positive. These alterations in categoric and specific future thinking among depressed individuals appear to arise, at least in part, from contrast avoidance as an emotion regulation strategy. Taken together, these findings underscore the importance of considering retrieval process alongside cue valence when characterizing the nature of altered future thinking in depression, an approach that may prove valuable for future work in this area.
